**Why.** A green `test` check and a green local complement together cover the suite, but each was only somebody's word about a commit. A gate can accept neither until both are bound to one exact commit and tree by something a session cannot type.

**What changed.**

- New `scripts/ci-evidence.js`. For HEAD it checks, in order: the tree is clean; HEAD is the branch's upstream after a fetch; the repository is the remote's; the `test` check concluded with success; the app is `github-actions`, the check run links to a workflow run as its own job, and GitHub's record of that job (`actions/jobs/<check run id>`) is a job of HEAD in that workflow run; that workflow run (`actions/runs/<id>`) is of HEAD and was a push, or a pull request's run that names a pull request of HEAD into the base branch; the base branch's tip, read from GitHub, is an ancestor of HEAD; the ledger compiles; the complement, which the command runs itself, is green; and afterwards the tree is still clean, HEAD is the same commit and the ledger's bytes are the same. The first fact that does not hold is printed after `REFUSED:` and nothing is written.
- On success it writes `.prawduct/lane-evidence/ci-<sha>.json` (git-ignored): the repository, the check (run id, workflow run id, workflow name, the event that started the run, app, conclusion, completion time), HEAD and its tree, the base branch and tip, the ledger's path and sha-256, the complement's verdict with every entry and what this host measured for each requirement, and when it was written. Every field is one the run read; a reading that lacks one refuses.
- It takes no argument naming a commit, a repository, a check, a ledger or a result. `--base <branch>` is the one input and defaults to the remote's default branch.
- `test/ci-evidence.test.js` fakes git and GitHub for each refusal, and also drives every git step through real git against a temporary repository with a real remote. The second kind was added after the command's first real run refused at step 2: it asked git for `refs/heads/<branch>@{upstream}`, which git accepts only with a short branch name, and no faked test could see that. The upstream's ref is now read by name (`for-each-ref %(upstream)`). No test calls GitHub.
- `scripts/ci-complement.js`: new exported `runComplement(io)`, which holds what `main` did after parsing its arguments and returns the verdict as a value, with `problems` listing why nothing was judged. `main` calls it and prints what it printed before; its tests pass unedited.
- `docs/lane-ci-evidence.md`: the command, its checks, the file's fields, what the file does not prove, and the proposed lane rule under a heading that says PROPOSED, NOT IN FORCE.
- `docs/prawduct-feedback.md`: the draft of the upstream report, marked NOT SENT.

**What an operator sees.** Nothing in the product. A Builder has a new command.

**What does not change.** What `prawduct-hook test-status` accepts, and every lane rule. The command never touches `.prawduct/.test-evidence.json`.

**Limits.** The file does not show that the job named `test` ran the suite and not something else of that name: it does not read the workflow's definition. It is for before the merge: GitHub names no pull request on a run once the pull request is merged, or for one from a fork, and the command then refuses. It is a wide run (it runs the complement). The default base comes from the clone's record of the remote's default branch; a clone without one must name `--base`.

## 2026-10-12 — #2322: read a check as one branch sees it

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2322-integrator-merge-queue -->

#2322 Chunk 05a. Branch `feat/2322-05a-branch-check-reader`, from `origin/main` `7a3e1826`. The issue stays open. Nothing calls the new reader: the queue still lands nothing.

Visual change: no

**Why.** One commit can carry `test` runs made for several branches. `lib/github-checks.js#readCheck` answers the newest of them, so a run made for another branch could be read as `main`'s or as a candidate's. Seen on `abe00a40`: `main`'s own run was green and a later run for a probe branch was cancelled, so the newest read `cancelled`. The decider ruled that a branch is judged only by a run whose check suite names that branch.

**What changed.**

- `lib/merge-queue-github.js`: new `readBranchCheck(repo, branch, sha, name)`. Two bounded reads: the commit's runs of that name (`filter=latest`), then its check suites. A run counts only when its suite's `head_branch` is exactly the branch; the newest of those is answered, so a re-run of the branch's own run replaces it whatever it concludes. The kept runs are read by `readCheck`'s own exported `interpret`, so the answer is `readCheck`'s (with the app and, for an Actions job, the workflow run) plus `suiteId`.
- **Never stands in:** a run whose suite names another branch, or gives no branch or one that is not a string. If none is left the answer is `not-found`.
- **Fails closed, `unavailable`:** either read failing; either list at its page size, or with a count that disagrees with its rows, or holding a row of another commit; a suite with no usable id or an id given twice; a run of that name whose suite is not in the list.
- `lib/github-checks.js` is not edited.
- `docs/merge-queue.md`: the function's row in the table of calls and the sentence under it (the file was added to this chunk with the Integrator's leave).
- Tests in `test/merge-queue-github.test.js`, against seven new recordings in `test/fixtures/merge-queue-github/` (reads only; its README says what each is).

**What an operator sees.** Nothing.

**Limits.** No re-run on `main` existed to record; the re-run recording is a pull request branch's. A suite was seen to name its branch about three and a half hours after the branch was deleted, and not later than that.

## 2026-10-12 — #2323: register, read and cancel a check watch

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2323-check-watch-wake -->

#2323 Chunk 02. Branch `feat/2323-02-check-watch-api`, from `origin/main` `59e9fa7e`. The issue stays open. Nothing reads GitHub for a watch and nobody is woken: that is chunk 03, which is held.

Visual change: no

**Why.** Chunk 01 built the watch's records, its derived state and the reader of a commit's check, and nothing could reach any of it. This chunk is the surface: a session registers a watch for its own project with a due time, reads the state the server derives, and cancels.

**What changed.**

- New `lib/check-watch-api.js`: four handlers. The caller is resolved by `control-auth#resolveControlCaller`. `register` and `cancel` are a verified live launch's alone, for its own project; the Operator is refused (`CHECK_WATCH_LAUNCH_ONLY`). `list` and `show` answer the project, the Operator and a project that shares a group with it; to anyone else a watch or a project's list is a 404, the same body as for one that does not exist. Every answer carries the state from `check-watch-state#derive` at the time of the read, `open`, and `monitored: false`. No answer carries a launch id, in a watch or in a fact's detail.
- **The repository is not taken from the request.** `register` reads the `origin` remote of the project's own folder with `git` (bounded at four seconds, a seam for tests) and takes it only when `stranded-check#repoOf` reads it as a github.com repository. No remote, or one elsewhere, is `409 CHECK_WATCH_REPO_UNKNOWN`; a read that did not finish is `503 CHECK_WATCH_REPO_UNREADABLE`. It is not a boundary against the project's own session, which can write that folder's git config; the doc and the module say so.
- **The due time is minutes from now on the server's clock** (`dueInMinutes`, a whole number from 1 to the limit). A time named by the caller is not taken.
- `server.js`: the four routes under `/api/tc/check-watches`, through one wrapper that turns a `WatchError` into the `{error, code}` body and anything else into `503 CHECK_WATCH_UNAVAILABLE`.
- `lib/tc-verbs.js`: verb `check` with `watch`, `list [--project <id>]`, `show`, `cancel`. `unknown` is rendered as "neither failed nor done" with its reasons in words. Each answer from a server that says `monitored: false` ends with a line saying nothing reads GitHub for the watch and nobody is woken. The verb is in the roster and in `tc`'s usage and **not** in the list panes are primed with (`primed: false`): the primer section had less room left than the verb's name takes. So `CLAUDE.md` and the prime golden files are unchanged, and a test holds the tracked block to the generator's output.
- New `docs/check-watch.md`: what a watch is, the four states and the reasons for `unknown`, who may do what, the routes, `tc check`, the limits, each refusal code, and that neither table is pruned.
- Tests: new `test/check-watch-api.test.js` (every route as the project, a group neighbour, an outsider, the Operator, an unbound caller, a spoofed launch and an ended launch, over an in-process server; one test reads a real folder's remote with real `git`), new `test/tc-check-verb.test.js`. `test/launch-binding-routes.test.js`: the two state-changing routes are named and neither is reachable unidentified. `test/ecosystem-primer.test.js`: one pin, the verbs left unprimed with the candidate switch on are now `queue` and `check`.

**What an operator sees.** Nothing unless a session runs `tc check`. A session that does is told on every answer that the watch is a record only.

**Limits.** No read of GitHub, no wake, no alarm at the due time, no expiry: a watch reads `waiting` until its due time and `unknown` after it. Nothing checks that the pull request exists or that the commit is its head. A held lane can register and cancel. `derive` is unchanged, so the `past-due` point carried from chunk 01's review is still open.

## 2026-10-12 — #2321: the check reader says which app made a run, and which workflow run it belongs to

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2321-ci-as-suite-evidence -->

#2321 Chunk 02a. Branch `feat/2321-02a-check-app`, from `origin/main` `fd53bbea`. The issue stays open: one chunk follows, and the lane rule it asks for is not in force.

Visual change: no

**Why.** Any app with write access to checks can post a run named `test`. Before a reading of CI can be evidence, the reader has to say whose run it read. `readCheck` kept the conclusion and dropped the app.

**What changed.**

- `lib/github-checks.js`: a `running` or `concluded` answer now carries `app`, the slug of the app that made the newest run of that name, and `workflowRunId` when that run is a GitHub Actions job. The request's projection gains `.app.slug` and `details_url`; it is still one request.
- A run that does not say which app made it reads `unavailable`, so no answer names a run without its app. Only the newest run of the name is read for it, as for everything else.
- `workflowRunId` comes from the run's own `details_url`, only when the app is `github-actions`, the link has exactly the shape `https://github.com/<repo>/actions/runs/<id>/job/<id>`, `<repo>` is the repository asked about (compared without regard to case) and the job it names is the check run itself, as it is for a real job. That link is written by whoever made the run, so another app's run gets no workflow run whatever its link says. Any other link leaves the field out and is not an error.
- The reader reports the app and does not judge it: a `test` run made by another app is answered with that app's slug. Refusing it is the evidence command's, in the next chunk.
- `ACTIONS_APP` is exported. `interpret` takes the repository as a fourth argument; without it no workflow run is read.
- `test/github-checks.test.js`: the app and workflow-run cases, and the whole-answer assertions gain the two fields. `test/fixtures/github-checks/`: `commit-with-actions-run.json` and `workflow-run.json` are real answers captured for this repository's commit `06ac528f`; `named-check-projected.json` was captured again through the reader's own request, which now asks for the two fields.

**What an operator sees.** Nothing.

**Callers.** `lib/check-watch-store.js` and `lib/merge-queue-api.js` copy the fields they use out of the answer and neither compares a whole one; `lib/merge-queue-github.js` uses only `invalidRequest`. None was edited and their tests pass as they were.

**Limits.** Neither field proves a run is a workflow's own job: a workflow step holding `checks: write` can post a check run of its own, which GitHub attributes to `github-actions` too, with a link it chooses. The id is what the link says. Read on 2026-10-12: `repos/<repo>/actions/jobs/<check run id>` answers a real job with its `run_id` and answers 404 for a check run that is no job; the evidence command in the next chunk makes that read before it calls a run CI's. A check run whose app GitHub leaves out now reads `unavailable` where it read its conclusion before; the real answers captured here all carry one.

## 2026-10-12 — #2321: one command runs the tests CI skips on your own machine, and fails unless every one owed there passed

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2321-ci-as-suite-evidence -->

#2321 Chunk 01b. Branch `feat/2321-01b-ci-complement`, from `origin/main` `037083de`. The issue stays open: one chunk follows, and the lane rule it asks for is not in force.

Visual change: no

**Why.** A green `test` check never claims the tests `test/skip-ledger.json` names. Before CI can stand in for a full local suite, something has to run exactly those tests on a Builder's machine and refuse to read a skip as a pass. The decider ruled that what is run and what is expected come from the ledger and from measurements of the host, and from nothing a caller types.

**What changed.**

- New `scripts/ci-complement.js`. It reads the ledger through the audit's `compileLedger`, measures every value on `LEDGER_REQUIREMENTS` on this host, runs `test/*.test.js` with one `--test-name-pattern` for each entry (the last ` > ` segment of its `match`), and judges the junit report against each whole `match`. One line for each entry: ran and passed; ran and failed; expected here and skipped, with the test's own reason; matched nothing; not expected here, naming the value that is false; unmeasurable, naming the value. The last line counts the entries that ran here and names each that did not, with why. `--list` prints what each entry requires and what the host measures, and runs nothing. `--junit <path>` says where the report goes. No option leaves an entry out or reads another ledger.
- A case belongs to an entry when the pattern matches its own path or the path of a suite enclosing it, read from the report's nesting. Four entries name a suite that the runner skips whole; where it runs, the report holds its tests under it (the Integrator's ruling on the first step's finding).
- `engine-cli:each` is judged per test: a skip is accepted only for an engine measured absent.
- A listed value with no measurement is unmeasurable, never true, and a test pins that the complement measures exactly the audit's list.
- `test/api-uploads.test.js` (added to the chunk by the Integrator, two tests): each now makes the `.uploads` folder it sets to mode 000. Selected alone they failed with `ENOENT`, because an earlier upload test in the file had made the folder. No assertion changed.
- New `test/ci-complement.test.js`. `docs/lane-ci-evidence.md` gains how to run the complement, how it selects and judges, how to read each line, and how each value is measured. `CONTRIBUTING.md` (added by the Integrator, one sentence) points at it.

**What an operator sees.** Nothing in the product. A Builder has `node scripts/ci-complement.js`.

**Limits.** It is a wide run: every test file is loaded. It changes nothing a gate accepts; no rule lets it, with CI, stand in for the suite yet. `scripts/test-skip-audit.js` and the ledger are unchanged. `caddyfile:live` reads the install's own home, so it measures the machine's live install and not a scratch one.

## 2026-10-12 — #2322: make a merge queue, submit to it, read it, withdraw, record the Operator gate

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2322-integrator-merge-queue -->

#2322 Chunk 04. Branch `feat/2322-04-queue-api`, from `origin/main` `ad6f2cbb`. The issue stays open. Nothing lands yet: assembling and landing are the next chunk.

Visual change: no

**Why.** Chunks 01 to 03 and 02b built the queue's records, its GitHub calls and the CI trigger, and nothing could reach any of it. This chunk is the first surface: the Operator makes the queue and names its owner, the owner submits an exact pull request head with its gates, and anyone entitled reads the order.

- `lib/tc-verbs.js`: verb `queue` with `status`, `submit`, `show`, `withdraw`. It is in the roster and in `tc`'s usage and **not** in the verb list panes are primed with (`primed: false`): adding it took the ecosystem primer section five characters past its cap, and that cap is not this chunk's to raise. So `CLAUDE.md` and the prime golden files are unchanged.
- `docs/merge-queue.md`: who may do what, the routes, the Operator's command, `tc queue`, each refusal code, the limits; and two sentences owed from chunk 02b's review.
- `.prawduct/project-state.yaml`: the API versioning and error-model decisions, recorded for the first time: unversioned `/api`, `{error, code}`.
- Tests: new `test/merge-queue-api.test.js` (every route as the Operator, the owner, a group neighbour, an outsider and an unbound caller, over an in-process server with GitHub faked at the module's seam), new `test/tc-queue-verb.test.js`, new `test/merge-queue-release-guard.test.js` (reads `release.yml` and fails if it watches a path the list does not name, loses its `paths` filter or gains another trigger). `test/launch-binding-routes.test.js`: the four state-changing queue routes are named and none is reachable unidentified. `test/ecosystem-primer.test.js`: one pin, the verbs left unprimed with the candidate switch on are now `queue`.

**From the reviews.** The Operator's command in the doc uses the dashboard's `tcFetch`: a bare `fetch` carries no CSRF token and a signed-in install refuses it, which the first draft of the doc missed; a test now makes a queue as a signed-in Operator. `submit` asks the HOLD gate twice, before the GitHub reads and immediately before the write. The `ENTRY_ID_UNUSABLE` refusal reads its own withdrawal's answer and says which happened. The test of what a refusal says reads every refusal body answered in the file, in place of one that searched the source.

**What an operator sees.** Nothing until they make a queue with the command in `docs/merge-queue.md`. After that the owner project's session can run `tc queue submit` and anyone in its group `tc queue status`.

**Limits.** Nothing assembles a candidate or merges. A held owner can still withdraw. A coordinator mid-rotation is not fenced from these routes. The Operator's command has not been run against a live install. The store mints entry ids, so an id that could not name a branch is caught after the entry is written and withdrawn at once, not refused before.

## 2026-10-12 — #2231: a Codex that cannot answer its version probe is asked once, then not again for 60 seconds

<!-- prawduct: type=bugfix | scope=codex-probe-stall-2231 -->

#2231 Chunk 01, the plan's only chunk. Branch `fix/2231-01-codex-probe-memory`, from `origin/main` `bffc06c4`. The issue stays open.

Visual change: yes — the launch refusal for such a Codex has a longer message.

**Why.** The launch's version probe is synchronous and may run 5 seconds, and the server has one thread. Measured on `bffc06c4` with counting tests on unchanged code: a project launch or relaunch ran `codex --version` twice (the capability read, then the app-server start or the legacy command) and three times when the app-server could not start. A Project Master ensure ran it once. A non-answer was never kept, so every launch, ensure and summon retry paid the wait again.

**What changed.**

- `lib/startup-control-codex.js`: `probeForLaunch` is the launch's probe and returns `{version, enginePath}`; `probeVersionSync` returns its version. An executable that fails, times out or prints no version is remembered for `PROBE_UNANSWERED_MEMORY_MS` (60 seconds), in the process only, by its resolved path and a stamp of the file (modification time, size, inode, device). Inside that time it is not run and the probe carries `retryInSeconds`. A changed file, the time passing, or a clock that went backwards asks again. An unreadable file and a bare command name are never remembered. A version that was read is never kept. `isolateLaunch` and `prepareLaunch` take `probe` and use it when it is of the same executable. `judgeLaunchCommand` words the `version_unknown` refusal for a probe that was not run; the code and the recovery text are unchanged.
- `lib/startup-control.js`: `resolveForLaunch` returns the probe it made.
- `lib/sessions.js` (added to the chunk's files by the Integrator): `_prepareStartupChannel` hands that probe to `prepareLaunch` and returns it; `launchSession` hands it to `_isolatedLegacyLaunch`, which takes one more optional argument. With no probe handed on, each step asks for itself as before.
- `docs/engine-guide.md`: the behaviour, and the adapter contract's new optional parts.
- Tests: `test/startup-control-codex.test.js` (the memory, with a fake clock and real files), `test/startup-control-launch.test.js` (probes per launch on the native, legacy and fallback paths and on relaunch; two launches of a Codex that cannot answer), `test/master.test.js` (one case, added to the chunk's files by the Integrator: one probe per ensure, and again on the next).

**What an operator sees.** With a Codex that hangs, the first launch waits once (5 seconds, not 10) and is refused as before. For the next 60 seconds a launch that needs that Codex is refused at once, and the message says it was not asked again and in about how many seconds it will be. Other engines' launches no longer wait behind it.

**Limits.** The first ask still stops the server for up to 5 seconds. The asynchronous probe on the request path is unchanged and neither reads nor clears the memory. A Codex that starts answering without its file changing is refused until the 60 seconds end.

## 2026-10-12 — #2322: a candidate is merged on a stage branch, so only the candidate push starts CI

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2322-integrator-merge-queue -->

#2322 Chunk 02b, built after chunk 03 and before chunk 04. Branch `feat/2322-02b-stage-prefix`, from `origin/main` `b66190cb`. The issue stays open.

Visual change: no

**Why.** `createCandidate` created the `tc-queue/` branch at the base and then merged into it. Since chunk 03 the `test` workflow runs on every push under `tc-queue/`, and the first of those two pushes is on a commit that is `main`'s own. Seen on the real repository after chunk 03 merged: a `tc-queue/` branch created at `main`'s tip put a second `test` run on that commit, and a branch at the same commit under `tc-queue-stage/` started nothing. So every entry would have cost two suite runs and changed what the `test` check on `main`'s tip reads. The decider ruled two prefixes.

**What changed.**

- `lib/merge-queue-github.js`: `createCandidate(repo, ref, baseSha, headSha)` keeps its signature. It now creates `tc-queue-stage/<name>` at the base, merges the head into it, reads the merge commit back and requires the same tree and parents, creates `tc-queue/<name>` at that commit (the only write under `tc-queue/`), and deletes the stage branch. `created` is answered only when all of that was read and the stage branch is known gone. After a conflict or nothing to merge the stage branch is deleted and no candidate branch exists. `ref-exists` says which name was taken (`existing`). Every answer carries `refCreated` and new `stageLeft`; a stage branch that could not be deleted turns any answer into `unavailable`. New `discardCandidate(repo, ref)` deletes both branches, always attempts both, and says what happened to each. `deleteRef` accepts one name under either prefix and nothing else. New `STAGE_PREFIX`, `isStageRef` and `stageRefFor`, the one place the stage name is built.
- `test/merge-queue-github.test.js`: the candidate cases rewritten for the sequence, each step failing alone by refusal and by timeout, the two name collisions, the read-back disagreeing, `discardCandidate`, and the two-prefix limit of `deleteRef`. The earlier cases that pinned a branch left at the base after a conflict are replaced, because that behaviour is the thing removed: a conflict now leaves no branch.
- `test/fixtures/merge-queue-github/`: seven exchanges recorded from one real candidate made and discarded by the module itself; the two recordings of calls the module no longer makes are removed. The README says which sitting each file is from.
- `test/workflow-queue-trigger.test.js`: one added case. No name under `STAGE_PREFIX` is matched by the workflow's push list.
- `docs/merge-queue.md`: the call table, the limits, what an answer means, what was checked against GitHub (now with what the two later sittings saw), and how a candidate is tested, moved below it.

**What an operator sees.** Nothing: still no route, verb or screen.

**Checked against GitHub.** One sitting on 2026-10-12, allowed once by the decider, through the module's own functions: base `43e13e78`, head `ccfe717f`, branches `tc-queue-stage/probe-20261012-092611` and `tc-queue/probe-20261012-092611`. One `Tests` run started, a push run for the candidate branch on the candidate commit `48774bab`; none on the base, on the head or for the stage branch, read for 70 seconds; the run was still in progress after the stage branch was deleted; the candidate's tree equals the tree of `main`'s own merge of the same two commits. The run was cancelled, both branches were deleted and read back gone, and `main` was not written.

**Not verified.** A candidate's run through to its end (the one run was cancelled once read). Merging a pull request, as before. Other apps installed on the repository recorded check suites against the stage branch; none is named `test`, and what they do with a stage branch was not looked into.

The workflow file GitHub uses for a push is the one on the pushed commit, so this can be seen only after the line is on `main`: one throwaway `tc-queue/probe-*` branch, pushed and deleted, on the Integrator's word after the merge. A candidate's run does not execute the cache-bump step, which is limited to pull requests. **Found by the review and not settled here:** `createCandidate` makes two pushes to the candidate branch, the first at the tip of `main`, so the new trigger may start a second `test` run on `main`'s own tip each time a candidate is made; the reader the landing will use takes the newest run of that name on a commit. Not observed. The same throwaway branch answers it, and the plan's chunk 05 carries the decision.

## 2026-10-12 — #2059: the wake holds for one monitor interval after a launch's thread first reports idle

<!-- prawduct: type=bugfix | scope=codex-wake-2059 -->

#2059 Chunk 01 of two. Branch `feat/2059-01-wake-settling-hold`, from `origin/main` `3d7ef5fe`. The Architect's D2 (A123, 2026-10-07), built first by its later ruling (A175 R9). #2059 stays open: this chunk verifies no Codex version and does not make a Codex newer than 0.156.1 wakeable.

Visual change: no

**Why.** A Codex session is woken on its app-server's word that its thread is idle, and that word can arrive a moment before the TUI has drawn the screen it will rest on. Measured on Codex 0.156.1 (the cold-start probe A123 ruled on, finding F4): the channel answered idle 4.16 s into a launch and a pane sample 10 ms later still drew the `model: loading` header over an empty composer, a screen the pane gate does not refuse once it is handed idle. What kept a nudge out of that screen was the idle streak, a debounce on the pane that exists for another reason and may be retuned. The Architect ruled that the debounce constant is not to be the only thing closing the overlap.

**What changed.**

- `lib/medusa-wake.js`: the first idle answer for a session's channel and launch is timestamped where the answer is stored, under the same key the answer carries. Until `ENGINE_SETTLING_MS` (one monitor interval) has passed since then, `_judgeSession` holds as `engine-thread-settling` before the pane is read, on the ledger and on the peer route like its sibling codes, and in observe-only mode as a `wake_blocked` fact. A relaunch's new channel, or a channel that went away and came back, starts the hold again. A later busy or unknown answer on the same channel does not: a turn ending is not a launch. A clock that steps backwards holds.
- The idle streak the pane gate asks for is read through a seam (`_internal.idleTicksRequired`), so a test can change it and show the hold does not lean on it. Its value is unchanged.
- `PEER_REASON_MEANINGS` gains the code's meaning; `lib/medusa-delivery-disposition.js` classes it `actionable`, `wait`.
- `docs/engine-guide.md` and `docs/user-guide.md` describe the hold and the code.

**What an operator sees.** The first mail a Codex session gets after its launch, or after a server restart, is announced one monitor interval (five seconds) later than before; a sender asking in that interval is told `engine-thread-settling`. Later mail is not delayed. Engines not judged by a native channel are unaffected.

**What it does not do.** It refuses no screen. Codex 0.156.1's opening screen, trust prompt and update prompt, and the opening screens of 0.159.0 and 0.161.0, stay on the known-unsafe list in `test/medusa-wake-codex-fixtures.test.js`: a nudge is still typed into one that is up when the interval has passed, if the channel answers idle over it. Refusing them is #2187's next chunk. Nothing in the launch's own pane gate (`_awaitPaneReady`) changes, and this is not evidence about the automatic startup fire.

**Tests.** `test/medusa-wake-engine-activity.test.js`: the recorded overlap replayed at its measured times with the streak set to one, so only the hold is in the way; one full interval and not a millisecond less; a thread idle for longer is nudged on the streak alone; busy then idle does not restart it; a replaced channel and a returned channel do; three values of the streak, none of which removes it; a standing clock and a backward one hold; a Claude session is never held to settle. The file's ticks now move the clock as the timer does, and its Codex nudges take one more tick. `test/medusa-wake-codex-fixtures.test.js`: its fresh-idle cells are fed a settled answer and keep their outcomes, the known-unsafe ones included; a new block holds every pane of every captured version on the first idle answer. `test/medusa-delivery-disposition.test.js`: the code's row. `test/medusa-watchdog.test.js`, added to the chunk's files by the Integrator for one case's setup: that case drives a Codex session through the real wake on a clock that stood still, which never ends a timed hold, so its ticks now move the clock by `ENGINE_SETTLING_MS`. No assertion in it changed and `lib/medusa-watchdog.js` is untouched.

**Not verified.** The plan's look at the reason on the peer route from a scratch server was not done: the code is reached only by a Codex session with a live native channel and waiting mail, which a scratch server gets only by launching Codex and sending it mail, the wake experiment the Architect's ruling does not permit on fake-key evidence. In its place a test reads the held verdict through `peerReachability`, the function the peer route answers from. The hold on a real cold Codex launch: by the Architect's ruling nothing is woken on fake-key evidence, so this is tests only until the deploy. No screen shows a wake reason's meaning (`public/` was searched), so there was no browser look.

## 2026-10-12 — #2322: the merge queue's reads and writes on GitHub, checked against the real repository

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2322-integrator-merge-queue -->

#2322 Chunk 02 of five. Branch `feat/2322-02-merge-queue-github`, from `origin/main` `29354a46`. One module, its tests and fixtures, and the first section of `docs/merge-queue.md`. No route, no `tc` verb, no store or schema change: nothing an operator can reach.

Visual change: no

**Why.** The queue tests a pull request merged with the current `main` on a branch of its own, then merges the pull request. Both are GitHub's work, so the queue needs one bounded place that asks GitHub and never reports what it did not read, and the design's assumptions about GitHub had to be tried on GitHub before anything was built on them.

**What changed.**

- New `lib/merge-queue-github.js`: `readPull`, `readRefTip`, `readCommit`, `readPullFiles`, `readCompareFiles`, `createCandidate`, `deleteRef`, `mergePull`, each a `gh api -i` call with a time and output budget. The HTTP status is read from the response itself, so a conflict (409), nothing to merge (204), a branch that is already there (422) and a branch that is gone (404) are named answers and everything else is `unavailable` with the reason. `deleteRef` and `mergePull`, one write each, say when `unavailable` whether GitHub refused it (`applied: false`) or its outcome is not known (`applied: null`). `createCandidate` is two writes and says instead whether a branch now exists (`refCreated`), which is what tells a caller to delete one.
- Writes are held to the queue's own branches: `createCandidate` and `deleteRef` refuse any branch that is not one name under `tc-queue/` before `gh` runs. `mergePull` always sends a merge commit pinned to a full head commit and cannot send squash or rebase. `CANDIDATE_PREFIX` is exported for the workflow trigger test of a later chunk.
- The two file-list readers for the release refusal answer `unavailable` unless the list is known to be whole: every page read and the row count equal to the pull request's own count and under GitHub's 3000 cap; a comparison under the 300 cap whose second commit descends from the first. A rename gives both paths.
- `lib/github-checks.js` is used as it is: the check-run reader is not copied, and its definition of a well-formed repository and commit id is the one this module asks.
- New `test/merge-queue-github.test.js` and `test/fixtures/merge-queue-github/`: the tests replay the exchanges the module itself recorded, and assert the module sends the recorded arguments.
- New `docs/merge-queue.md`: which GitHub calls the queue makes and what each may change.

**The probe (allowed by the decider's `2322 probe ok`; the Integrator's `probe base 6ee1f14b`, `probe refusals ok`).** Run once on 2026-10-12 through the module's own functions with this session's `gh`. Branch `tc-queue/probe-20261012-071816` was created at `6ee1f14b` (`main` before PR #2331), `1aa8f5de` (that PR's head) was merged into it as `bb4d7904`, and it was deleted; `git ls-remote origin` then printed no `tc-queue/*` line and `main` was still `29354a46`. Four calls that write nothing were made on the same branch: creating it again (422), the same merge again (204), a merge that conflicts, of `cc3e8364` (409), and deleting it again (422); the branch tip read `bb4d7904` before and after the first three.

**What the probe did not keep.** The candidate's check-runs read went through `lib/github-checks.js`, whose fixtures are its own; its answer (`not-found`) is recorded here and in the PR, not as a fixture. The two refusal merges were sent by the probe script and not by a module function, which only merges into a branch it has just made; the fixtures' README says which two.

**What the probe showed.** The candidate's tree is `a09b4f59`, the tree of `main`'s own merge of the same two commits (`29354a46`): GitHub's branch merge and its pull request merge gave the same files. That is one pair of commits, and the queue still compares the trees after every landing. The candidate had no `test` run: CI does not run on a `tc-queue/` branch until the workflow chunk lands, so the assumption that a branch made with this `gh` login starts the workflow is still untested.

**Departures from the plan, each told to the Integrator before the probe.** The probe branch started at `6ee1f14b`, not the tip of `main`: merging an older `main` commit into the tip answers 204 and makes no merge commit. `mergePull` uses `PUT /pulls/{n}/merge` with `sha` (the same pin as `gh pr merge --match-head-commit`) because it answers the merge commit as JSON, which the landing's tree comparison needs. Acceptance criterion 4 is amended in the plan: `mergePull`'s three answers are written from GitHub's documentation, in `pull-merge-DOCUMENTED.json`, which says so, and are to be replaced by the real exchange from the queue's first landing.

**A defect caught by the tests before the first commit.** The first version checked a commit id through a helper whose parameter had a default, so a call with no head commit passed the check and would have sent `sha=undefined`. Root cause: one helper served "repository only" and "repository and commit" by defaulting the commit. They are two functions now, and the refusal tests pass `undefined` for every commit argument.

**Not checked.** A real pull request merge. A file list long enough to page or to reach a cap (those tests are built on the recorded row shape). A pull request from a fork. GitHub rate limiting (a 403 reads as `unavailable`, refused). The recorded pull request of the files test changes five files, none renamed.

## 2026-10-11 — #2187: whole Codex dialog fixtures, and a known-unsafe list kept by version

<!-- prawduct: type=bugfix | scope=wake-gate-2187 -->

#2187 Chunk 01 of two. Branch `feat/2187-01-whole-dialog-fixtures`, from `origin/main` `6ee1f14b`. Fixtures and their test only: no product code, no schema, nothing an operator sees.

Visual change: no

**Why.** The issue says the wake's pane gate, given a fresh idle answer from the engine's channel, reads Codex's update prompt and folder-trust prompt as a bare prompt, and that the merged fixtures hide it because they were trimmed to the dialog's own rows. Before the gate is changed, the fixtures have to show what a pane really holds.

**What was read.** Each dialog was read again on 2026-10-11 the way the wake reads a pane (`tmux capture-pane -p -S -15`), in a 100x30 pane on a private tmux server, with a fresh Codex home and a fake API key. Codex 0.159.0 and 0.161.0 were installed into a throwaway prefix under the session's scratchpad; 0.156.1 is the host's binary, run with a private home and never updated.

| Version | Screen | Trust prompt | Update prompt |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.156.1 | main, 11 rows of history | `at-prompt` | `at-prompt` |
| 0.159.0 | alternate, no history | `no-prompt` | `no-prompt` |
| 0.161.0 | alternate, no history | `no-prompt` | `no-prompt` |

The verdicts are `_assessPane` on `6ee1f14b` with the shipped Codex profile and a fresh idle, with the cursor withheld and with its position given.

**The issue is right about 0.156.1 and wider than the evidence for the other two.** 0.156.1 draws on the main screen, so the header box and the composer row it opened with are still in the read, above the dialog, and the gate's text check accepts that stale row. 0.159.0 and 0.161.0 draw on the alternate screen: the dialog is the whole pane, no composer row is above it, and the gate holds it. Their merged fixtures were already the whole screen and their `pane-no-prompt` pin was right. The issue's sentence "on every Codex startup screen the pane contributes no refusal" holds for 0.156.1 only. Reported to the Integrator before anything was committed; it ruled `2187 per version` and the plan's first two acceptance criteria were rewritten to match.

**What changed.**

- `test/fixtures/codex-panes/codex-0.156.1.json`, `codex-0.159.0.json`, `codex-0.161.0.json`: `trustDialog` and `updateDialog` are the new whole reads. Each pane carries its own capture date, the screen it was drawn on, and a note of the folder, the launch and what the read did not cover. 0.161.0 gains an `updateDialog`: a newer version exists now, so it draws one.
- `test/medusa-wake-codex-fixtures.test.js`: the known-unsafe list is keyed by version. 0.156.1 names the opening screen and both dialogs; 0.159.0 and 0.161.0 name the opening screen only, and their dialogs stay pinned as held. A new block checks that each dialog read says its screen, runs to the dialog's footer, holds a stale composer row exactly when it was drawn on the main screen, and is on the known-unsafe list exactly when it holds one. The table of what each pane reads with no at-rest marker follows, by version.

**Not checked.** The cursor's styled row was not captured, so the cursor-given verdicts used the footer row's plain text (0.156.1) and an empty row (the other two). No signed-in account: with a fake key the header says `model: loading` and no model list arrives. One pane size. The 0.155.1 set is rows excerpted by hand and was not read again; its `dialog` pane says nothing about a whole read of that version. Whether a live channel can answer idle while any of these screens is up is not a fixture's question and is not answered here.

**For Chunk 02.** The later-glyph-row refusal (A123's D3) is needed on the main-screen version; on the alternate-screen versions it must not break a hold that already works. The Integrator sent the finding to the Architect and the decider.

## 2026-10-11 — #2323: the check-run reader, the watch records and the derived state (schema v65)

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=2323-check-watch-wake -->

#2323 Chunk 01 of three. Branch `feat/2323-01-check-watch-records`. No route, no verb, no timer, no file under `public/`: nothing an operator can reach yet, and nothing reads GitHub unless a later chunk calls the reader.

Visual change: no

**Why.** A session that is waiting on CI has no way to be told when it finishes: it polls, or it sits. The issue asks that a session name a check on an exact commit and a due time, and that TangleClaw record the result itself and wake the session once. This chunk is what the other two stand on: how one check on one commit is read from GitHub, where a watch and its history are kept, and how a watch's state is derived from the record.

**What.**

- `lib/github-checks.js` (new): `readCheck(repo, sha, name)` asks GitHub through `gh` for the runs of that name on that commit and answers `not-found`, `running`, `concluded` (with the conclusion, the run's id and when it completed) or `unavailable` with the reason. The newest run of a name wins. A malformed repository, commit or name is refused before `gh` runs. Anything not understood is `unavailable`, never a guess. Written against real responses captured from this repository (`test/fixtures/github-checks/`), one of them the answer to the reader's own filtered request.
- `lib/check-watch-state.js` (new): the fact kinds, the refusal codes, the limits (20 open watches a project, a due time at most 24 hours ahead), `derive(watch, now)` and `requestIdFor(watchId, kind)`. No I/O.
- `lib/store.js`: schema **v65**. Two tables, `check_watches` and `check_watch_facts`, created on a fresh install and by the v64→v65 migration, which reads the stored DDL back before the version advances.
- `lib/check-watch-store.js` (new): every read and write over those tables. Each write is one transaction with its fact.

**Enforced by the database itself.** One open watch per project, repository, commit and check name; a result written whole or not at all; each once-only fact written once (`registered`, `resolved`, `woke`, `wake-undeliverable`, `alarmed`, `alarm-undeliverable`, `cancelled`, `expired`); a `woke` or `alarmed` fact always naming its request id, its exchange and its workspace; a history that refuses UPDATE and DELETE.

**What the decider's rulings needed from the tables.** The wake and the alarm must each be sent under one deterministic request id, reused on a retry, so a restart between the switchboard accepting a message and the fact being written cannot send it twice. The tables carry that: a watch id is random text and never reused; `requestIdFor` makes the request id from the watch id and the message kind alone, and a test pins its form against the shape the switchboard accepts; the sent facts are once-only and carry the exchange id. A watch that is never resolved ends by itself 24 hours after its due time (`2323 expire 24h`): the `expired` fact kind and the `expired_at` column are here, the open-watch index and the per-project limit leave an expired watch out, and `derive` reads it `unknown` with an `expired` reason. Nothing writes `expired_at` in this chunk.

**Beyond the plan's column list**, each told to the Integrator before the suite: the last read's time and outcome are columns on the watch, so `derive` takes the watch and the time and not the facts; `read-recovered` beside `read-failed`; `alarm-undeliverable` beside `wake-undeliverable`; the open-watch index covers the repository too; the sent facts carry the request id and the recipient workspace as columns.

**Found while building.** A CHECK written as `last_read_detail IS NULL OR last_read_outcome = 'failed'` let a detail through on a row with no read at all, because a comparison with NULL is not false to SQLite. The migration test caught it; the CHECK now names the missing outcome.

**From the review.** `derive` called a record or a time it could not read `past-due`; it now says `unreadable`. `registerWatch` threw on a finite due time beyond what a Date holds, where the store promises a refusal; such a number is now refused. A test ties the check-name limit the store's CHECK is built from to the one the reader accepts.

**Not checked.** Nothing calls these functions yet, so no route, verb, timer or live server was exercised. The reader was run by hand against GitHub for a passed check, a missing name and an unknown commit; the tests replace `gh` at its seam. No real v64 database from an install was migrated.

## 2026-10-11 — #2186: Codex 0.156.1 is listed in the folder-trust allowance, on a signed-in probe

<!-- prawduct: type=feature | scope=codex-trust-2186 -->

#2186 Chunk 01 of two. Branch `feat/2186-01-codex-trust-allowance`, from `origin/main` `e4dfaf47`. No schema change and no product code change: profile data, two guides, and two tests.

Visual change: yes

**Why.** PR #2225 built the allowance that lets a Codex startup fire go into a folder with no trust entry when a pane witness sees an empty composer, and shipped it switched off: `startupControl.remoteTrustPrompt.absentOn` was empty until one signed-in fire had been probed through the real `--remote` path (Architect A154). Until then a session launched into a new or renamed folder got no first turn.

The copy was deleted when the probe ended and neither it nor the original changed. The list was switched on in the scratch home's copy of the profile.
- A plain new folder: no trust dialog; the Fire was refused `trust_required` with the list empty, and accepted and applied with 0.156.1 listed; the row carried the dispatch note; Codex's config held no trust entry afterwards.
- A new folder with its own `.codex/config.toml` and hooks: refused `engine_not_ready` while Codex's update prompt was up; accepted and applied once it was skipped. No hook ran and the folder's config was not applied; Codex's own warning says those are disabled until the project is trusted.

- `docs/engine-guide.md`, `docs/user-guide.md`: the allowance is active on 0.156.1, what was measured, what still blocks, and that the folder's own Codex config and hooks stay off until the folder is trusted. The guide's status-row sentence now lists the keys the witness treats as a footer's.

- `test/startup-control-codex.test.js`: the one case that pinned the shipped list as empty now pins it as listed and asserts the fire goes with its note. Added to the chunk with the Integrator's leave. What the old case proved for an empty, other or missing list is still proved by the case above it.

- [DECISION: the version is listed although the automatic launch fire was not exercised signed-in. | Both probed sends were the operator's Fire: on a Codex home with the default status line the launch's pane gate never saw its at-rest marker and recorded `pane_not_ready` before the allowance was reached. The two callers share one rule and the existing tests cover both; the marker is #2059's subject. The evidence text and both guides say so.]
- [DECISION: the guide's sample `startupControl` block keeps `"absentOn": []`. | It is the shape for any engine, not Codex's profile.]

The two narrow widths were the page in a same-origin frame, because the browser window would not shrink. Recorded as VRF-004; the Operator's own look is owed after the deploy.
